# Capital Flow Evidence Ledger Index

## What This Is

This is the first machine-readable evidence ledger for the capital-flow work.

The ledger starts from the current analysis files and extracts claim-linked metrics for the capital platforms and the first operating chain, power and grid.

Capital platforms:

- BlackRock
- Blackstone
- Apollo
- KKR
- Brookfield
- Ares
- Carlyle

Power and grid operating companies:

- Constellation
- NextEra
- Vistra
- NRG
- AEP
- Duke
- Exelon

Industrial distribution and built-environment suppliers:

- WESCO
- Ferguson
- Core & Main
- United Rentals
- Fastenal
- Grainger
- Builders FirstSource
- Sherwin-Williams

Internet software control companies:

- Cloudflare
- Akamai
- Fastly
- Zscaler
- DigitalOcean

Healthcare access and care-delivery companies:

- UnitedHealth
- Cigna
- DaVita
- Option Care
- Addus
- BrightSpring
- Enhabit

`analysis/company-first-principles/data/capital-flow-evidence-ledger.csv`

The pass works in both directions:

- Claim to evidence: start with CF-004 and CF-005, then ask which exact metrics and source documents are required.
- Evidence to claim: start with Apollo and Ares source numbers, then rewrite the claims to match what the numbers actually prove.

Important status note: the main ledger remains a seeded scaffold. The new verification log records primary-source-backed metrics without forcing source URLs into the original ledger schema.

The acquisition manifest is the next bridge. It lists source documents to pull, where to store them locally, which claim IDs they support, and which metrics should be extracted.

The bank denominator pass adds Federal Reserve H.8 series so the private-credit claim can be tested against bank-lending stocks instead of only against company growth metrics.

The BDC borrower pass adds ARCC as a borrower-facing Ares-managed vehicle, so the claim can be tested with portfolio company count, funded investments, first-lien mix, floating-rate mix, yield, and non-accrual metrics.

The BDC industry-lane pass adds a funded-destination view. ARCC and OBDC now supply official reconciled industry subtotals, while BXSL supplies explicit top-ten industry exposure percentages.

The borrower matching workbench adds the next test for the bank-displacement claim. It converts named borrowers and industry lanes into evidence requirements: sponsor, use of proceeds, prior debt source, current lender group, and bank facility role.

The borrower source-of-funds pass applies that test to Auctane/Stamps.com, Guidehouse, and Medallia. It shows four different claim outcomes: private-credit acquisition finance, primary-source bank-credit takeout, direct bank competition for a buyout financing, and private-credit workout/control risk. The borrower transaction fact-extraction file now has `17` rows, including the Stamps.com closing 8-K credit-termination marker, the Medallia `1.8B USD` debt-commitment marker, and a blocked Guidehouse/Yahoo source check. The claim-strength matrix prevents those cases from being overstated as one generic bank-displacement claim.

The borrower credit-document pursuit log records the newest search outcomes. Auctane improved from intended facility payoff to confirmed closing termination. Medallia improved from transaction announcement plus proxy repayment language to a primary-source debt-commitment amount. Guidehouse did not improve because the Yahoo mirror returned only a rate-limit page.

The Apollo/Athene liability-to-credit pass extracts `10` rows from Apollo's Q2 2026 financial supplement. The primary-source numbers now support a narrower CF-004 claim: Apollo/Athene has a large retirement-liability inflow channel, a large invested-asset base, growing alternative-investment exposure, spread-related earnings, and credit management fees. Exact private-credit origination linkage still needs asset-class and borrower-destination detail.

The Apollo/Athene FY2025 asset-quality pass adds `24` SEC 10-K rows. It connects the Q2 flow claim to annual-report credit lanes and liability structure: `938.0B USD` total AUM, `302.1B USD` direct-origination AUM, `282.7B USD` asset-backed-finance AUM, `392.2B USD` Athene Accounts AUM, `386.5B USD` Athene Accounts fee-generating AUM, `292.414B USD` net invested assets, `48.6B USD` of floating-rate investments, `45.1B USD` of floating-rate liabilities, `8.6B USD` of capital available to deploy, ACRA `37%`/`63%` economics, `80%` to `100%` internal ceded reinsurance quota shares, and surrender-charge metrics. This strengthens the asset/liability bridge but still does not prove borrower-level bank displacement.

The KKR/Global Atlantic insurance pass adds `11` rows from KKR's Q2 2026 earnings release. The first non-Apollo manager comparison supports the broader mechanism: Global Atlantic had `220B USD` of AUM, including `164B USD` of credit AUM, plus `62B USD` of Ivy and sponsored reinsurance vehicle AUM. This broadens CF-004 from Apollo-only evidence to a two-manager pattern, while leaving origination composition and borrower destination as the next proof layer.

The KKR/Global Atlantic Q2 2026 10-Q pass adds `36` SEC HTML rows behind the AUM claim. It extracts `189.204B USD` of insurance investments, `205.499B USD` of insurance policy liabilities, `88.057B USD` of AFS fixed maturity securities, `48.754B USD` of mortgage and other loan receivables, `4.028B USD` of six-month net investment income, `671.094M USD` of mortgage/other loan credit-loss allowance, `228.1M USD` of below-investment-grade AFS gross unrealized losses, `296.4M USD` of mortgage loans 90 days or more past due or in foreclosure, `45.203B USD` of total mortgage loans by property type, and `9.2B USD` of FHLB funding-agreement pledged assets. This adds balance-sheet and credit-quality context, while showing the channel is not bank-free because FHLB funding is bank-adjacent.

The Brookfield Wealth Solutions insurance pass adds `16` rows from Brookfield's Q2 2026 supplemental and shareholder letter. Brookfield gives the clearest allocation bridge so far: `190.715B USD` of total insurance assets, `167.297B USD` of invested insurance assets, LTM gross inflows of `20B USD`, LTM net inflows of `8B USD`, and an investment portfolio allocation of `33%` private credit, `49%` public credit, and `5%` private funds. This turns RNQ-007 into a three-manager pattern, with credit quality and borrower destination as the next proof layer.

The Blackstone Credit & Insurance pass adds `19` official web-rendered PDF rows. Direct local download attempts returned Cloudflare challenge pages and are logged as blocked, so the extraction table records source URLs and line references instead of claiming local PDF evidence. The Blackstone row changes the mechanism: it does not prove owned insurance liabilities, but it does show third-party insurance-client capital flowing into Blackstone-managed credit strategies, including `7.5B USD` of insurance SMA inflows inside infrastructure and asset-based credit strategies.

The insurance-credit channel comparison adds a `4` row normalized bridge across Apollo/Athene, KKR/Global Atlantic, Brookfield Wealth Solutions, and Blackstone Credit & Insurance. It turns the finding into two-direction research logic: evidence-to-claim shows what the extracted numbers safely prove, while claim-to-evidence lists the missing borrower documents, statutory schedules, rating-agency exhibits, and bank denominator series required for stronger claims.

The derived claim metrics pass adds `36` formula-backed rows. It converts extracted source numbers into auditable ratios and implied dollar values, including Brookfield's implied `55.208B USD` private-credit exposure, KKR/Global Atlantic's `74.55%` credit-AUM share, KKR/Global Atlantic's `25.77%` mortgage-and-other-loan share of insurance investments, Blackstone's `75.76%` insurance-SMA share of cited infrastructure and asset-based-credit inflows, and Apollo/Athene's `98.55%` Athene Accounts fee-generating AUM ratio. These rows explicitly state what each calculation supports and what it still cannot prove.

The next evidence coverage pass adds a `10` row source matrix for the next layer of proof. It maps each stronger claim to exact numbers, source paths, source entry points, acceptance rules, and next actions across Apollo/Athene, KKR/Global Atlantic, Brookfield Wealth Solutions, Blackstone Credit & Insurance, Auctane, Medallia, Guidehouse, NAIC/statutory filings, rating-agency reports, and bank-denominator datasets.

The Atwell bank-replacement test adds a borrower-level pursuit log for the Ares selected borrower with the strongest prior-bank evidence. Atwell has a disclosed `200M USD` Bank of America-led senior credit facility in 2024 and a disclosed 2026 Ares/Antares private-credit acquisition-financing trail. A follow-up search added Antares transaction-display criteria and a Weil web-rendered term-loan/revolver clue, but no payoff, termination, amendment, rating report, or UCC record. The K-FITS and FSK holder extractions upgrade the evidence with SEC-filed holder-level positions: K-FITS reports `4.002M USD` funded principal and `0.488M USD` unfunded commitment; FSK reports `3.2M USD` funded principal and `0.4M USD` unfunded commitment. The current claim is deliberately bounded: Atwell is a live bank-replacement candidate with reported holder-level private-credit exposure, not confirmed bank-displacement proof.

The AeriTek SEC holder crosswalk applies the same method to a commercial refrigeration and foodservice equipment borrower. It finds ARCC, Kayne Anderson BDC, and ASIF holder rows tied to AeriTek-related borrower names. The latest visible non-duplicate 2026 rows disclose at least `71.3741M USD` of AeriTek-related fair value across ARCC, Kayne Anderson BDC, and ASIF. The safe claim is acquisition-platform borrower-destination evidence, not total acquisition financing or bank-replacement proof.

The MAI SEC holder crosswalk applies the same method to a wealth-management and advisory-services borrower. It finds ARCC, ASIF, New Mountain Private Credit Fund, and New Mountain Guardian IV holder rows tied to MAI Capital-related borrower names. The latest visible non-duplicate 2026 funded rows disclose at least `35.2233M USD` of MAI-related fair value plus a separate `12.401M USD` undrawn commitment row. The safe claim is wealth-management consolidation borrower-destination evidence, not total facility size or bank-replacement proof.

The next work program converts the open questions into eight execution lanes: bank-replacement proof, SEC holder expansion, facility-size reconstruction, capital-source-to-vehicle mapping, insurance statutory quality, bank denominator matching, operating destination economics, and claim-strength governance. The scorecard currently rates Auctane as strongest for confirmed bank-facility termination, Medallia as strong but incomplete, Atwell as a moderate unproven replacement candidate, and broad bank-disappearance language as unsupported.

The all-company triage pass scans all `519` company packets and ranks them by capital-flow evidence density. It finds `190` high-signal rows with score >= `80`, classifies the primary lane for each company, and shows that the highest-signal broad expansion set is not only private credit. The first-pass distribution is led by capital intensity/capex, power-grid/project finance, acquisition finance, debt facilities, insurance/retirement capital, asset-backed/securitization, and a smaller explicit private-credit/platform lane. The correct use is work allocation, not proof: take the top `25` to `50` rows, then pull annual reports, quarterly reports, credit agreements, rating reports, and source-of-funds documents.

The power/grid claim dashboard adds a `6` row interpretation layer on top of the `41` row first extraction and `17` row source upgrade. It grades five subthemes: regulated rate-base growth and utility buildout, merchant and contracted power, midstream infrastructure and fuel logistics, buildout service layer, and digital infrastructure adjacency. The safe current claim is that power/grid is a capital-absorption lane where money is moving into physical capacity and bottleneck relief. The unsafe claim is that AI alone explains all power value creation or that every backlog/capital-plan dollar becomes attractive returns. The next proof layer requires project economics, regulatory approvals, contract terms, outside EIA/FERC/ISO/rate-case denominators, and backlog-to-cash conversion.

The power/grid outside-denominator plan adds a `12` row source matrix for moving the lane from company-source proof toward outside-denominator proof. It maps each dashboard claim to external data families: EIA-861, FERC Form 1, FERC Form 714, EIA-860, EIA-923, ERCOT GIS, PJM Data Miner, EIA gas/liquids datasets, state utility commission dockets, financing documents, contractor conversion tables, and ABM/CACI adjacency filings. The purpose is to test whether management claims survive comparison against delivered demand, plant additions, queue status, regulatory approval, financing structure, and cash conversion.

The power/grid utility denominator map adds the first implementation layer for the regulated buildout claim. It creates a `20` row join-key workbench across NextEra, Duke, AEP, and Exelon. The important change is methodological: the research no longer treats a parent utility as a single denominator. It splits the companies into operating utility/entity rows, states, likely regulator needs, EIA/FERC lookup status, balancing-authority or ISO hints, and next extraction targets. That is required before comparing capital plans and load claims to EIA-861, FERC Form 1, FERC Form 714, state rate cases, and ISO/RTO evidence.

The AEP/Duke load reconciliation workbench adds an `18` row bridge from official-source-backed company metrics to denominator-ready tests. The AEP/Duke regulatory docket extraction pass adds `9` first-pass regulatory extraction rows. The AEP/Duke approved-recovery bridge pass adds `22` rows that execute the power/grid operating-return question. The Cheniere train source-use-return bridge pass adds `24` rows that execute the energy operating-return question. The Cheniere project cash-waterfall source target pass adds `16` rows executing `CFE2EGUQ-007`. It sharpens the money-movement answer: capital moves through company cash generation, equity-funded growth capital, entity debt buckets, and credit/funding capacity into LNG trains and terminals; those assets produce externally verifiable cargoes through Sabine Pass and Corpus Christi under long-duration customer contracts. The strongest evidence includes FY `2025` revenue of `19.98B USD`, FY `2025` DCF of `5.29B USD`, Q2 `2026` revenue of `5.73B USD`, Q2 adjusted EBITDA of `1.80B USD`, Q2 DCF of `1.17B USD`, Q2 company/DOE cargo match at `184` cargoes, Q2 DOE volume of `640991.63 MMCF`, about `90%` contracted SPL/CCL production, about `15` years weighted-average SPA/IPM life, CPC Taiwan SPA volume up to `1.2 mtpa`, CCL Stage 3 `94.1%` completion, Train 6 substantial completion, FY `2025` growth capital of `2.6B USD` with `2.3B USD` equity-funded, Q2 `2026` growth capital of about `1.1B USD` with `219M USD` equity-funded, and entity debt/contract denominators including SPL, CQP, CCH, parent notes, `29.0B USD` debt-plus-interest, and `290.6B USD` estimated executed-SPA revenue. The Cheniere train cash-waterfall source test pass adds `16` rows executing `CFCRSWO-003`. The Cheniere entity DSCR waterfall bridge pass adds `10` rows that upgrade Cheniere from contracted LNG output bridge visible to entity-waterfall-mechanics visible. The 519-company batch evidence refresh pass adds `18` rows that convert the completed case-study bridges into repeatable extraction gates. The 519-company pilot refresh pass 2 adds `35` selected pilot rows from the `71` row lane queue. The lane candidate queue pass turns the all-company triage into `71` lane-specific primary-source queues. The all-company scale-up operating model adds a `14` row bridge from broad coverage to deep proof. It answers the scale question directly: track the whole `519` company universe as a radar, deepen the `190` high-signal rows as priority candidates, and push the `71` selected lane rows into theme/subtheme-specific extraction. The model defines separate proof packages for private credit, insurance and retirement capital, power/grid/project finance, debt refinancing, acquisition finance, asset-backed finance, and capital-intensive buildout. It also defines the depth ladder, cross-sector tracking logic, end-to-end research questions, and operating rules for avoiding status-bucket mixing.

The cross-theme claim status dashboard adds a `15` row decision layer for writing and checking thesis claims. It standardizes status buckets across the research program: queued, announced, filed, approved, financed, under construction, in service, trend-proven, and thesis-grade. The rows cover private-credit platform scale, borrower-level destination proof, bank replacement, insurance liability-to-credit channels, asset-quality and risk migration, customer-backed load, recoverable utility capital, generation procurement, service backlog, midstream logistics, refinancing, acquisition finance, asset-backed finance, capital-intensive buildout, and outside-denominator governance. Its main use is to prevent overclaiming: each row states what the evidence proves, what it does not prove, and what source would move the claim one status higher.

The funding-type hypothesis map adds `10` rows that explain why different capital-intensive companies may use different financing containers. It frames the working model as `funding type follows asset type`: diversified networks can use corporate revolvers, commercial paper, and notes; contracted LNG assets can support entity-level debt; reusable rental assets and receivables can support ABL and securitization; backlog/acquisition platforms may need flexible revolvers and letters of credit; regulated assets can be repaid through rates; and contractor backlog may be customer-funded upstream. The page is intentionally hypothesis-grade: each row has proof metrics and disproof tests rather than treating the funding explanation as proven.

The refinancing event ledger pass 1 adds `15` packet-derived, source-pointer-backed debt/refinancing rows across `10` companies: PBF, Liberty Broadband, Devon, Matador, Coterra, CNX, Ovintiv, Cenovus, Wheaton, and Enhabit. It moves the debt/refinancing row in the cross-theme dashboard from `queued` to `filed` first-pass evidence. The strongest rows include PBF's ABL exit and about `802M USD` of senior-note refinancing, Liberty Broadband's `966M USD` debenture-put settlement and approximately `359M USD` Charter term loan, Coterra's staged repayment of acquisition-related term-loan debt, Ovintiv's asset-sale-funded May `2028` senior-note redemption, Cenovus's MEG term-loan repayment, Wheaton's Antamina term-loan/revolver financing, and Enhabit's February `2026` amended credit agreement. The pass preserves the boundary: it still needs debt footnotes, credit agreements, indentures, lender lists, pricing, covenants, collateral, and extinguishment accounting before any row becomes credit-agreement-grade.

The acquisition finance ledger pass 1 adds `12` packet-derived, source-pointer-backed M&A/control rows across `10` companies: Core & Main, Verizon, Sherwin-Williams, Lowe's, Roblox, Expand Energy, Rio Tinto, Packaging Corporation of America, Silgan, and Phillips 66. The asset-backed finance ledger pass 1 adds `14` packet-derived rows across `10` companies: Synchrony, Pool, Arrow, Global Industrial, MSC, Target, Dollar General, Zebra, Honeywell, and Henry Schein. The pass preserves the rule that ordinary working capital is not asset-backed finance unless the filing shows collateralized funding or credit-risk transfer.

The capital-intensive buildout ledger pass 1 adds `15` packet-derived rows across `15` companies: Jacobs, Steel Dynamics, Eaton, Energy Transfer, Sterling Infrastructure, Teledyne, AECOM, Knife River, Nucor, KBR, Verizon, Reliance Steel & Aluminum, United Rentals, Cheniere, and ONE Gas. The capital-intensive project denominator workbench adds `18` rows that convert the source-backed priority rows into outside proof tests. The capital-intensive energy project denominator pass adds `14` first-pass external denominator rows for ET and Cheniere. The capital-intensive industrial denominator pass adds `16` rows for URI and Sterling. URI now has first-pass outside context from Census QSS/BEA/FRED: NAICS `5324` machinery/equipment rental and leasing revenue was `27471M USD` in Q1 `2026`, up `6.8%` year over year, trailing-four-quarter broad NAICS `5324` revenue was `115007M USD`, and U.S. private fixed investment in construction machinery was `64.723B USD` in `2025`. Sterling now has Census construction denominators showing a mixed broad market and a stronger mission-critical pocket: June `2026` private nonresidential construction was `745339M USD` SAAR, down `4.7%` year over year; manufacturing construction was `172674M USD` SAAR, down `21.4%`; office construction was `132750M USD` SAAR, up `12.5%`; and processed Census/BLS data-center construction spending was about `4.356776304B USD` in June `2026`, up `44.3%` year over year. The safe claim is that URI and Sterling now have external denominator context; the unsafe claim remains full fleet-return, backlog-funding, or project-return proof.

The capital-intensive industrial conversion pass adds `16` rows that test whether URI and Sterling convert denominator exposure into company mechanics. URI rows show H1 `2026` operating cash flow equal to `112.8%` of gross rental capex, `2.040B USD` of net cash rental-equipment investment after sale proceeds, Q2 `2026` rental revenue equal to `16.2%` of OEC as a simple productivity proxy, OEC up `1.32B USD` or `5.9%` from year-end `2025`, Q2 fleet productivity up `3.4%`, and liquidity of `2.999B USD` including ABL and accounts receivable securitization facility availability. Sterling rows show acquisition revenue contribution equal to `21.5%` of Q2 `2026` revenue, combined backlog equal to `4.8x` Q2 revenue and `1.38x` the 2026 revenue-guide midpoint, CEC/Stone Ridge signed-backlog plus unsigned-award contribution of `2.56B USD` or about `45.6%` of combined backlog, H1 operating cash flow less capex of `258.375M USD`, and a July `2026` revolver expansion to `1.5B USD`. The safe claim is now conversion-supported but still bounded: URI converts fleet capital into productive cash-generating access, while Sterling converts project demand into a layered backlog/cash funnel with material acquisition contribution.

The capital-intensive project funding map adds `20` rows across ET, Cheniere, URI, and Sterling. It separates company cash capacity, facility capacity, direct funding split, debt-stack context, and funding gaps. ET rows show Q2 adjusted EBITDA equal to `88.2%` of the FY2026 growth-capex guide midpoint, Q2 DCF equal to `45.0%` of that guide midpoint, unused five-year credit-facility availability equal to `65.4%` of that guide midpoint, and `68.393B USD` of long-term debt less current maturities. Cheniere rows show the strongest direct split: Q2 growth capital was about `19.9%` equity-funded and H1 growth capital was about `24.8%` equity-funded, with `22.632B USD` of long-term debt context. URI rows show operating cash flow covering `112.8%` of H1 gross rental capex and liquidity equal to `59.4%` of the current 2026 gross rental-capex guide midpoint. Sterling rows show `258.375M USD` of H1 operating cash flow less capex and the July `2026` revolver expansion to `1.5B USD`. The safe claim is first-pass funding capacity and funding-split evidence; project-level source-of-funds remains open.

The big-picture remaining-work map adds `12` rows that keep the whole research program aligned to the full chain: capital source, funding vehicle, destination company, asset/project, use of proceeds, operating conversion, cash/return, durability, outside denominator, disproof tests, claim governance, and scale-up system. Its safe claim is process-level: the remaining work is not just more company pages, but a requirement to prove `capital source -> funding vehicle -> company -> asset/project/backlog -> operating output -> cash/return -> durability -> safe claim`.

The promoted-claims and overclaims pass adds `15` rows that translate the current research into disciplined writing rules. Each row states the sentence that is safe to say now, the stronger sentence that is not yet allowed, the current evidence tier, the promotion test, and the disproof signal. This is the claim-control layer for the capital-flow thesis: visible BDC fair value is not total borrower debt, one bank-takeout case is not broad bank replacement, customer backlog is not automatically risk-free funding, and one quarter of evidence is not yet a durable trend.

The capital-intensive credit agreement and project-finance pass adds `20` rows that move the same ET, Cheniere, URI, and Sterling work from funding capacity toward credit-stack proof. ET rows show a `5.00B USD` Five-Year Credit Facility, potential `7.00B USD` accordion capacity, `1.21B USD` of outstanding facility borrowings including `1.12B USD` commercial paper, `3.00B USD` of January `2026` senior notes used to refinance existing indebtedness, and a `1.00B USD` January `2026` note redemption funded with cash and commercial paper. The URI ABL borrowing-base definitions pass adds `32` rows from the July `2025` Fifth Amended and Restated Credit Agreement. The URI owned-rental segment economics pass adds `40` rows from the filed Q2 `2026` 10-Q. Segment capex was `2.279B USD` for general rentals and `817M USD` for specialty. The URI owned-rental segment trend pass adds `40` rows from filed annual and interim tables. It moves the owned-rental/segment evidence from current-period visibility to trend support: owned-equipment rentals were `80.0%` of FY `2025` equipment-rentals revenue, down from `82.5%` in FY `2023`, while specialty rose from `27.0%` to `33.6%` of annual equipment-rental revenue and from `33.1%` to `38.5%` of annual equipment-rentals gross profit. The same pattern appears in H1 `2024` to H1 `2026`, where specialty rose from `30.4%` to `36.1%` of equipment-rental revenue and from `37.9%` to `41.1%` of equipment-rentals gross profit. The boundary matters: specialty gross margin compressed from `48.9%` in FY `2023` to `43.6%` in FY `2025`, so this is `owned-rental-segment-trend-visible`, not segment ROIC or margin-expansion proof.

The URI segment asset-return proxy pass adds `30` rows from filed segment tables. It pairs segment equipment-rentals gross profit with segment total assets while preserving the non-ROIC boundary. Specialty produced more equipment-rentals gross profit per filed segment asset than general rentals in FY `2023` through FY `2025`, but the specialty proxy declined from `30.8%` in FY `2023` to `25.0%` in FY `2025`; general rentals declined from `15.8%` to `14.8%`. In H1 `2026`, specialty's annualized equipment-rentals gross profit / average segment assets was `26.4%` versus `14.7%` for general rentals, while specialty's average asset share rose to `28.0%`. The safe claim is `segment-asset-return-proxy-visible`; the unsafe claim remains ROIC, ROA, true utilization, segment OEC, operating-profit return, or growth/replacement capex proof.

The URI specialty margin driver boundary pass adds `22` rows from filed MD&A and segment tables. The filings name higher ancillary mix, specialty ancillary mix, inflation, normal cost variability, delivery costs, labor and benefits costs, used-equipment pricing normalization, Yak/acquisition mix, and Q2 `2026` specialty margin pressure as relevant source-trail drivers. The boundary is that none of these drivers is quantified into a full basis-point bridge, and none proves specialty ROIC deterioration.

The URI rate/time/mix source-boundary pass adds `18` rows. The boundary is that fleet productivity remains an aggregate of rental rates, time utilization, and mix, so the pass is `rate-time-mix-source-boundary-visible`, not rate/time/mix quantified. The transaction funds-flow source route pass adds `5` rows executing `CFRMSWO-011` across Matador borrowing base, Devon treasury allocation, PBF refinancing, Liberty Broadband holdco, and Plains tariff route. It identifies Distribution Inspection and Transmission Inspection as the strongest next targets: Distribution Inspection links `193199` actual projects, `61.5M USD` actual cost, and `15.942971M USD` final recovery; Transmission Inspection links `84056` actual projects, `66.3M USD` actual cost, and `23.595154M USD` final recovery. Vegetation has mileage/cost evidence but lacks final category recovery in the extracted set; Feeder Hardening has final recovery but needs AP-1 row-level output normalization. The safe use is category-output-recovery bridge language; the unsafe use is treating derived per-unit metrics as profit, customer collections, or project IRR.

The FPL Distribution Inspection attribution pass adds `13` proof-gate rows from already-extracted official PSC and NextEra source rows. It upgrades the category question by testing output, capital use, plant additions, recoverable expense, final category recovery, aggregate SPPCRC reconciliation, factor-order authority, carrying-charge context, FPL-wide cash context, customer receipts, category earnings components, financing source, and full attribution decision. The current result is `fpl-distribution-inspection-attribution-partial-upgraded`: the chain is strong through category-output-recovery, factor authorization, and category component attribution, but still fails customer-receipt and financing-source proof. The safe use is category-output-recovery-with-component-attribution language; the unsafe use is treating authorized recovery or FPL-wide cash lines as realized Distribution Inspection return.

The FPL Distribution Inspection component extraction pass adds `15` rows from final Form 7A and actual/estimated Form 7E schedule lines in official PSC PDFs. It shows the final `15.942971M USD` Distribution Inspection recoverable expense is made up of `10.481240M USD` equity component grossed up for taxes, `2.278910M USD` debt component, and `3.182821M USD` depreciation expense. Equity plus debt components total `12.760150M USD`, or `80.04%` of the final recoverable expense. The safe use is regulatory revenue-requirement component attribution; the unsafe use is treating those components as cash collected, financing source, shareholder profit, or project IRR.

The FPL Distribution Inspection receipts/financing boundary pass adds `12` rows from official PSC final true-up, factor-order, and Form 8A evidence. It shows aggregate final SPPCRC clause revenue of `804.620369M USD`, aggregate true-up collected/refunded of `65.318726M USD`, factor authority, total adjusted retail capital structure of `70.622413018B USD`, long-term debt of `22.907234278B USD`, short-term debt of `896.468170M USD`, common equity of `35.140912233B USD`, total weighted cost of `7.0330%`, and pre-tax total of `8.8822%`. The safe use is aggregate revenue and recovery capital-structure support; the unsafe use is claiming Distribution Inspection customer cash receipts or source-of-funds allocation.

The FPL Distribution Inspection customer receipt/source pursuit pass adds `12` rows that turn the remaining FPL hard gap into executable source gates. It keeps the claim at `fpl-customer-receipt-source-pursuit-ready-hold`: aggregate SPPCRC revenue, factor authority, Form 7A components, Form 8A capital structure, AP-1/AP-2 output, accounting-book tracking language, and the `2027` true-up route are visible, but no captured source yet allocates billed/collected customer cash or financing source to Distribution Inspection. The next required evidence is a category receipt schedule, billing determinant or category allocation workpaper, `2027` SPPCRC actual/estimated true-up support, PSC audit/discovery workpaper, or debt/treasury use-of-proceeds support.

The FPL Distribution Inspection customer receipt/source acquisition pass adds `14` proof-gate rows that execute end-to-end graph upgrade queue row `CFE2EGUQ-002`. It confirms the money movement bridge from regulated activity to recovery mechanics: `193199` actual Distribution Inspection projects, `61.500000M USD` AP-1 actual cost, `38.320627M USD` final expenditures, `22.263219M USD` final additions to plant, `15.942971M USD` final recovery, `10.481240M USD` equity component grossed up for taxes, `2.278910M USD` debt component, `3.182821M USD` depreciation, `804.620369M USD` aggregate SPPCRC clause revenue, `65.318726M USD` true-up collected/refunded, approved 2026 rate-factor authority, Form 8A capital-structure support, and FPL-wide cash context. It does not prove Distribution Inspection-specific customer receipts, source-of-funds allocation, project IRR, or earned shareholder return.

The FPL Distribution Inspection customer receipt/source test pass adds `16` rows executing cash-return source work-order row `CFCRSWO-002`. It confirms that the current local source set passes physical-output, category-cost, final-expenditure, plant-addition, final-recovery, recovery-component, aggregate-clause-revenue, aggregate-trueup, rate-factor-authority, recovery-capital-structure, and FPL-wide cash-context gates. The decision is `customer-receipt-source-test-hold-with-regulated-recovery-proxy`: FPL remains the strongest regulated recovery bridge, but Distribution Inspection-specific customer receipts, billing determinant allocation, source-of-funds allocation, project IRR, and earned shareholder return are still not proven.

The FPL SPPCRC remaining source status pass adds `5` rows that keep the post-true-up gaps explicit. The factor order, AP-1/AP-2 exhibit summaries, FPL-wide earnings/cash context, category-attribution bridge, Distribution Inspection component attribution, and recovery capital-structure boundary are now extracted; the remaining open source class is SPPCRC category receipts, source-of-funds allocation, and optional AP-1 row-level project IDs. The safe use is proof-routing language; the unsafe use is treating order-level approval, program-output evidence, component attribution, capital-structure support, or FPL-wide cash context as realized SPPCRC category return.

The URI fleet return bridge pass adds `16` rows that execute the scorecard's top-ranked next proof. It upgrades URI from operating-conversion proxy to return-quality proxy by tying OEC, rental revenue/OEC, EBITDA/OEC, owned-rental mix, segment gross margin, segment capex intensity, segment asset productivity, cash coverage, used-equipment recovery, and collateral support into one bridge. The safe use is bounded URI return-quality language; the unsafe use is claiming asset-class ROIC, legal borrowing-base availability, growth-versus-replacement capex, utilization, or source-to-purchase allocation.

The URI fleet cash-yield source target pass adds `16` rows executing `CFE2EGUQ-006`. It answers the money-movement question for the fleet lane: capital moves into rental equipment through H1 `2026` rental-equipment purchase payments of `2.720B USD`, gross rental capex of `2.931B USD`, and net fleet cash investment of `2.040B USD` after `680M USD` of equipment-sale proceeds. The output side is visible through Q2 `2026` OEC of `23.8B USD`, Q2 owned-equipment rentals of `2.991B USD`, H1 owned-rental share of `78.1%`, Q2 rental revenue/OEC of `16.2%`, and adjusted EBITDA/OEC of `8.6%`. The return-quality proxy is strengthened by H1 Specialty equipment-rentals gross margin of `43.1%` versus General Rentals at `34.8%`, Specialty annualized gross profit / average segment assets of `26.4%` versus `14.7%`, OCF/gross rental capex of `112.8%`, OCF/net fleet cash investment of `162.0%`, and AR collateral-pool coverage of `125.8%`. The safe claim is fleet cash-yield proxy evidence; the unsafe claim remains fleet-class cash yield or ROIC proof until fleet-class OEC, true utilization, growth/replacement capex, segment operating profit, legal borrowing-base availability, reserves, advance rates, and source-to-purchase allocation are visible.

The URI fleet cash-yield source test pass adds `16` rows executing `CFCRSWO-004`. It converts the URI fleet target into a work-order decision: `fleet-cash-yield-source-test-hold-with-strong-company-fleet-proxy`. The answer is now clear at company/fleet proxy level: capital moves into rental equipment, owned assets produce most equipment-rental revenue, used-equipment sales recycle cash, company operating cash flow covers gross and net fleet investment, segment margins show return-quality differences, and receivables collateral supports the funding wrapper. The unsafe claim remains fleet-class cash-yield or ROIC proof because growth/replacement capex, fleet-class OEC, true utilization, rate/time/mix, segment operating profit, legal borrowing-base availability, reserves, advance rates, and source-to-purchase allocation are not visible in the current local source set.

The Sterling backlog return/risk bridge pass adds `20` rows that execute the scorecard's second-ranked next proof. It upgrades Sterling from strong-company-conversion proxy to backlog return-and-risk proxy by tying the July `2026` `1.500B USD` revolver, `600M USD` L/C sublimit, `646.306M USD` Q2 `2026` net contract-liability timing, `4.233618B USD` RPO, `4.3336B USD` signed backlog, `1.28B USD` unsigned awards, `2.56B USD` acquired backlog visibility, `258.375M USD` H1 `2026` operating cash flow less capex, and `6.7%` debt/RPO into one bridge. The safe use is bounded backlog return-and-risk proxy language; the unsafe use is claiming project-level return, full customer funding, actual L/C/surety usage, signed-equivalent unsigned awards, or acquired-backlog conversion quality.

The Sterling project cash collection source test pass adds `16` rows executing `CFCRSWO-005`. It converts the Sterling backlog target into a work-order decision: `project-cash-collection-source-test-hold-with-strong-backlog-cash-proxy`. Sterling answers the money-movement question at company/project-execution proxy level: customer/project commitments enter backlog and RPO, contract liabilities create favorable timing, OCF after capex shows company cash conversion, debt is low relative to RPO, and the July `2026` revolver/L/C/surety wrapper explains execution support. The unsafe claim remains named project cash-collection or project return because owner funding, project margin, retainage collection, bonded backlog, actual L/C usage, source-to-project allocation, and project return are not visible in the current local source set.

The theme/subtheme map adds the interpretive layer requested after the all-company scan. It organizes the `71` lane candidates into seven themes and their subthemes: private-credit platforms as routers, insurance and retirement liabilities as capital supply, power/grid/midstream/project finance, debt refinancing and balance-sheet repair, acquisition finance and consolidation, asset-backed finance and securitization, and capital-intensive buildout. Each theme now has candidate companies, proof packages, and disproof tests so the next reports can be detailed without becoming speculative.

The theme evidence program converts that map into `12` extraction programs. It specifies the company set, primary documents, outside datasets, first numbers to pull, derived-claim tests, acceptance rules, disproof tests, and next artifact for each major lane. This is the bridge from the broad thematic map to annual-report, quarterly-report, statutory, regulator, and transaction-document extraction work.

The power/grid first extraction pass starts executing that program. It adds `41` packet- and analysis-derived rows across Constellation, NextEra, Duke, Vistra, NRG, AEP, Exelon, ONEOK, Targa, MasTec, Dycom, Primoris, and Granite. The pass separates regulated utility buildout, grid-gateway load conversion, regulated wires reliability, merchant/customer-backed power, midstream growth projects, and contractor execution. Its central finding is that capital is showing up as GW, PPAs, electric-service agreements, load queues, utility capital plans, gas-turbine procurement, rate-base growth, transmission opportunities, recovery mechanisms, midstream capex, contractor backlog, fiber miles, transmission/distribution miles, and civil site work; the next step is to re-pull each row from the underlying 10-K, 10-Q, earnings release, investor deck, or regulator source.

The power/grid source upgrade adds `17` rows that connect selected first-pass metrics to official source-ledger packages for Constellation, NextEra, Duke, Vistra, NRG, AEP, Exelon, ONEOK, Targa, MasTec, Dycom, Primoris, and Granite. This moves the first utility/power wave, midstream wave, and buildout-service wave from Level 2 quantified exposure toward Level 3 official-source proof: releases, decks, supplements, 10-Qs, 8-Ks, and source-ledger IDs are now attached to the rows that support PPAs, ESAs, load queues, capital plans, rate-base-like capital, generation acquisitions, fleet availability, turbine procurement, affordability offsets, transmission opportunities, recovery mechanisms, midstream capex guidance, NGL/fractionation/export volumes, debt/liquidity, contractor backlog, book-to-bill, CAP, miles built, awards, and customer-backed power projects.

The Precinmac SEC holder crosswalk applies the Atwell method to a second Ares selected borrower. It finds SDLP and OBDC holder rows tied to Precinmac-related borrower names, including `274.884M USD` of funded fair value and `8.372M USD` of unfunded commitments in captured public schedules. The safe claim is borrower-destination evidence, not bank-replacement proof.

The Valcourt SEC holder crosswalk applies the same method to a third Ares selected borrower. It finds SDLP, ARCC, and Cliffwater holder rows tied to Valcourt-related borrower names. The latest visible non-duplicate 2026 rows disclose at least `459.6M USD` of Valcourt-related first-lien fair value across SDLP and ARCC. The safe claim is commercial-services roll-up borrower-destination evidence, not total facility size or bank-replacement proof.

The Ares borrower holder expansion pass adds `83` normalized rows across AeriTek, Atwell, Frontline Road Safety, MAI Capital, Precinmac, Relation Insurance, Sunvair, Valcourt, and Jiffy Lube / Premium Velocity Auto. It preserves row-level holder vehicle, source taxonomy, capital-channel group, borrower name, period, instrument, funded fair value, unfunded commitment, transaction context, facility-size status, bank-replacement status, safe claim, and do-not-claim boundary. The table now has `32` columns and zero unclassified source-channel rows after adding New Mountain Guardian IV BDC LLC and Cliffwater Corporate Lending Fund to the vehicle taxonomy. A second `9` row same-period summary selects one actual holder-dollar reporting period per borrower before summing fair value or commitments, while keeping Jiffy/PVA as transaction-context-only until a holder-dollar row is captured. The row-level audit table is intentionally not the promoted exposure total because it includes mixed-period and comparative rows; promoted borrower-level totals remain anchored to the master rollup: `1.1578648B USD` latest visible funded fair value, `1.2345575B USD` mixed-quarter visible funded fair value, `153.5020M USD` visible unfunded commitments, and `1.500B USD` hard transaction / bank denominator context.

The Ares borrower facility/use/cash proof stack pass adds `14` rows that execute end-to-end graph upgrade queue row `CFE2EGUQ-003`. It selects Frontline Road Safety as the strongest local Ares borrower test because it combines operating-borrower context, Bain sponsor transaction context, Ares lead-arranger/bookrunner evidence, `198.675M USD` of latest Q2 `2026` visible holder fair value, `76.6927M USD` of Q1 `2026` ASIF Ares-managed holder context, `54.300M USD` of visible 2026 unfunded commitments, first-lien instrument markers, and same-period exposure control. It holds on total facility size, use of proceeds, borrower cash generation, lender allocation, Ares funded allocation, debt-service coverage, and bank-role proof.

The Sunvair SEC holder crosswalk applies the same method to an aerospace MRO borrower. It finds ARCC, ASIF, and CADCX holder rows tied to Sunvair-related borrower names. The refreshed latest visible non-duplicate 2026 rows disclose at least `106.7154M USD` of Sunvair-related fair value across ARCC, ASIF, and CADCX. The safe claim is aerospace MRO borrower-destination evidence, not total facility size, CION source allocation, or bank-replacement proof.

The ARCC schedule parser pass now has two layers. Official industry subtotal rows reconcile to ARCC's reported total investments fair value of `29.349B USD`. The row-level parser still covers `22.456B USD`, or `92.5%` of reported non-controlled/non-affiliate fair value, so it remains a borrower-discovery map marked `parser-derived-needs-reconciliation`.

The OBDC schedule parser pass reconciles official schedule rows to OBDC's reported total investments fair value of `14.955B USD`, giving exact Blue Owl lane dollars across `30` industries.

The OBDC source-of-capital pass adds `44` rows from OBDC's Q2 2026 10-Q, Q2 2026 earnings exhibit, and captured FY2024 Precinmac holder evidence. It ties the Precinmac OBDC holder row to a Blue Owl-advised public BDC with `15.354604B USD` of assets, `7.903533B USD` of debt, `7.031759B USD` of net assets, `5.325B USD` of unsecured notes principal, `1.592073B USD` of CLO principal, `996.700M USD` of SPV asset-facility principal, a `4.000B USD` revolving credit facility, and `493.142569M` common shares outstanding. It also captures Credit SLF as a deeper joint-venture clue, including State Teachers Retirement System of Ohio's `12.5%` economic ownership interest, while explicitly not treating that as Precinmac-specific funding proof.

The BXSL industry percentage pass extracts `40` filed 10-Q industry rows that sum to `100.0%`, then converts them to `13.364B USD` of fair-value exposure using BXSL's filed total investments at fair value. The cross-BDC comparison now uses ARCC and OBDC official fair values plus BXSL official percentage-derived fair values.

The cross-BDC industry-lane comparison combines ARCC and OBDC official industry dollars with BXSL disclosed top-ten industry percentages converted to estimated dollars. It is strongest where ARCC and OBDC exact subtotals repeat the same lane, and directional where BXSL contributes estimated dollars.

The bank-credit trend pass adds one-year, three-year, and five-year growth windows for FRED bank-credit series. It shows bank credit is still growing in aggregate, so the private-credit thesis should be framed as a parallel-channel and transaction-lane shift, not a simple bank-collapse story.

## How To Read It

Each row connects:

`claim_id -> company -> source of capital -> routing platform -> funding instrument -> destination -> proof metric -> current metric value -> source file -> status`

The important field is `status`.

Current rows are marked:

`analysis-derived-needs-primary-source`

That means the numbers were extracted from the company-analysis notes, not yet re-pulled directly from the annual report, quarterly supplement, investor deck, or transcript.

## Why This Matters

This turns the capital-flow thesis into an auditable research program.

Instead of saying "private credit is growing" or "AI capital is becoming a power story," the ledger forces each claim to carry:

- proof metric
- disproof metric
- source target
- period
- numeric value
- confidence
- status

## Bottom Line

The ledger is the bridge between the narrative thesis and hard evidence.

The current version is a seeded scaffold for the broader capital-flow archive;
the integrated pilots now add primary-source-routed BHP/Wheaton, Apollo/Athene,
and retail proof gates. The [combined-investment-research method registry](combined-investment-research-method-registry.md)
and [current synthesis](combined-investment-research-current-synthesis.md)
are the authoritative entry points for that integrated layer.
